# output:
You're a student learning one subject deeply enough to both understand it and draw it beautifully in painterly pixel art: **the open sea and surf seen from oblique aerials: sea colour, swell, surf lines, foam around stacks, kelp**.

The Olympic coast and the San Juans are defined by the sea. voxsim's coast painter (on master, crates/vox-paint/src/biome/coast.rs) draws depth bands and surf, but beside a real WA Ecology oblique photo it reads as cartoon rings.

The work: how the real sea looks from the air and from shore:

- colour by depth, bottom and weather;
- swell refracting around headlands;
- surf breaking in lines and leaving drifting foam;
- white water around stacks and in surge channels;
- kelp beds as dark patches;
- all of it under overcast, sun and haze.

**A method we'd like you to try: close-up and iso together, from the first stage.** Develop the scene view (~0.1 m/px) and the 30° iso view (0.3–1 m/px) in lockstep: the same forms, palette and parameters, judged side by side at every stage, beside ground photos and oblique aerials. The elk-and-deer and grass-at-every-distance students did this, and their methods held together across scales. The conifers study didn't, and its close trees and its stands feel like two different techniques.

**Two halves, done together.**

- **Observe how it works.** Study the real thing from real sources: photos, video stills, field guides, scientific and historical literature. Find out what it is, why it looks the way it does, how it changes with season, weather and time, and how it behaves.
- **Translate it into pixels.** Find a method that draws it at every scale voxsim shows: close scene (~0.1 m/px), landscape, 30° iso and region (~1 m/px). Draw from what you observed.

The observation should be good enough that a simulation could use it, and the drawing good enough to sit beside the photos.

**Find your subject's model system first.** Before drawing, find the expert system that already explains how your subject looks and works, and build on it. The elk student built on Muybridge's plates and the naturalist's "four things". Natural scientists used to be trained to draw: a geologist could draw a flake, an outcrop or a fold with economy and truth. Naturalist painting guides were built on the same science. The studio library, ~/work/pixelart-studies/library/README.md, collects them:

- Lobeck's and Davis's block diagrams and Imhof's rock drawing for terrain;
- Ruskin and Holmes's Grand Canyon plates for rock and strata;
- the lithic illustration guide for a flake;
- Cole's *Artistic Anatomy of Trees* and Franklin's Douglas-fir stages for trees and forests;
- Tyndall for water, ice and glaciers;
- Howard and the WMO Cloud Atlas for clouds;
- Ruskin's and Speed's drawing courses.

Read the relevant ones closely, look at their plates, and say in LESSONS.md which model system you're standing on. If you find a better one, add it to the library README.

**Source reference data deeply, first.** The elk and deer

# The studio library: model systems

The elk and deer student succeeded largely because it didn't start from raw photos. It started from finished analysis:

- Muybridge's frame-by-frame plates;
- the naturalist's "four things that make a 6-pixel elk read".

Natural scientists used to be trained to draw: a geologist could draw a flake, an outcrop or a fold with economy and truth. Naturalist painting guides were built on the same scientific understanding. This library collects those traditions so studies can start where the experts finished, instead of doing a geology PhD from photographs.

**How to use it:**

- Before drawing, find your subject's model system here (or online, below) and read it closely.
- Then build on it, the way the elk student built on Muybridge.
- Open the PDFs and look at the plates; the drawings are the point.

Refetch anything missing with `./fetch.sh` (resumable). Files are also mirrored in S3 under `voxsim/shared/library/`.

## On disk (open access)

|---|---|---|
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/blockdiagramsoth0000armi_s8g3.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Block Diagrams* (1924).** How geologists draw landforms in 3-D: every landform type as a block diagram, with the drawing method. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/2015.205632.Geomorphology.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Geomorphology* (1939).** Landforms explained through his own drawings: glacial troughs, cirques, rivers, coasts, volcanoes. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/physicalgeograp00davigoog.pdf` | **W. M. Davis, *Physical Geography* (1898).** Davis's block diagrams: how landscapes form and age. |
| Terrain, rock at map scale | `terrain/2015.119840.Cartographic-Relief-Presentation.pdf` | **Imhof, *Cartographic Relief Presentation*.** The rigorous method for showing terrain by light, and Swiss rock drawing: cliffs rendered by structure with a few strokes. Probably the single most useful text for rock at landscape and region scale. |
| Rock, mountains | `rock/modernpainters04rusk.pdf` | **Ruskin, *Modern Painters IV: Of Mountain Beauty* (1856).** A painter's analysis of rock types, cleavage, crests, banks and stones, written for artists and illustrated with his own drawings. |
| Rock, strata at distance | `rock/holmes/*.jpg` | **William Henry Holmes, plates for the Grand Cañon Atlas (1882).** The masterpieces of American scientific landscape drawing: stratified cliffs and plateaus at great distance in a few values and stepped strata lines. Directly relevant to the Yakima basalt. |
| Rock, structure | `rock/reportongeology05duttgoog.pdf` | **G. K. Gilbert, *Geology of the Henry Mountains* (1877).** A classic illustrated geological report. |
| Rock, a flake | `rock/cifa_lithic_illustration_guide.pdf` | **CIfA, *The Illustration of Lithic Artefacts*.** How archaeologists draw a knapped flake: conventions for ridges, ripples and light. The drawing-a-flake skill itself. |

# Ready to use: the second studio (2026-09-26 night)

These are the techniques the second studio critic judged Ferrari-grade or ready to use. The critic ran 10 rounds on eight students, looking at 4–10× crops beside the students' photos and Ferrari's own pixels. Each item points to the student's folder, where the code, the lessons and the closing read in CRITIQUE.md live. Offered to the voxsim painting lanes as a resource, not a spec. None of the eight sets is at Ferrari's bar at 8× across the board; every student's "Where it stands" says so, and I agree with each of them.

**Masses first, for ground** (elements/ground: LESSONS "The rules to take even without the code"; sheets B and F; drop-in `ground.paint_ground(..., shadow_mask=)`). This is the studio's first answer to READY.md's main open problem.

- Decide two to four designed lit and shadow masses first, from off-frame casters and the landform.
- Keep shadow near black with 2–3 values, and put the colour in the light.
- Marks go inside a mass and never cross the terminator, except as a fringe of tips.
- Use ordered 2×2 dither for mid and far turns. Use runs only near.
- Best images: `sheets/F_scenes.png` (meadow bank, golden) and `sheets/B_landform_x_sun.png` (hummocks and contre-jour).

**Zoom handoff without pops** (elements/grass-lod: LESSONS §1–2, 6). Each tuft gets one stroke-size number, and every discrete choice is dithered against the tuft's own random numbers. Checked frame by frame across 100× zoom at four hours. The pan test and pop metric are reusable for any material.

**Conifers** (elements/conifers; best: `sheets/E_stand_x_hour.png`, `F_zoom_strips_1x.png`)

- Four PNW species at four ages with naturalist-grade form: nodding hemlock, cedar J-limbs with fluted buttress and candelabra, spruce curtains, the old Douglas-fir pillar.
- **Tier-top light** from V16: light on the upper surface of every tier on both sides of the stem, not on the sun-side half.
- Side-view stand relit at four hours by palette alone.

**Broadleaves** (elements/broadleaf; best: `stage5/contact_v7.png`, sheet A)

- Pacific madrone and Garry oak crowns. The oak comes from per-species elbow kinks and open crowns with limbs showing in the holes.
- Bark and moss: the checker-banded cylinder read from V30 ("pure / 50% / pure, never non-adjacent steps"), a moss sheath with drips and a seam, madrone peel.
- An LOD rule with no vanishing: a clump always emits a spray, and a spray always emits a mark.
- Phenology specific to each species.

**Sagebrush steppe** (elements/steppe; best: `hero/sage_flat_golden_3x.png`, `stage5/seasons_final`)

- The eye-level sage flat at golden hour, and the steppe's four seasons.
- The sage form: low, wider than tall, with vertical sprig streaks and a dark base.
- The bunchgrass tussock, and ponderosa bark.
- A method to lend everyone: box-downsample the photo to game resolution and paint from that.

**Forest understory** (elements/understory; best: `sheets/H_hour_x_canopy_scene.png`)

# Moving water meets rock: a student's notebook

Subject: whitewater, waterfalls, glacial lakes, meanders, surf around stacks, tide pools, tidal
currents and kelp, and intertidal bands, for the five voxsim places (Skykomish, Enchantments,
Yakima Canyon, Olympic coast, San Juans), at voxsim's camera (orthographic, 30 deg, scene 13 ppm /
landscape 2.6 ppm / region 0.9 ppm, the brief's ~1 m/px; voxsim's own region preset is 0.45).

Everything here is made by `src/` (Python, numpy + scipy). Every pixel is a palette index chosen by
a rule; the palette is the light. Photos are in `photos/<query>/` with `sources.tsv`.

*Work in progress. Written incrementally so a crash loses nothing (the laptop went down once
mid-study, 2026-09-26 morning; files were intact).*

---

## Part 1. Observation: how each water works (for simulation programmers)

Numbers are tagged: **[measured]** = I computed it from data or photos myself; **[source]** =
from the cited literature; **[mine]** = my reading, untested.

# output:
### 1.6 Intertidal bands (San Juans)

San Juan zonation by tide height above MLLW **[source: Kozloff, Life on Rocky Shores, San Juan
Nature Institute / UW Friday Harbor Labs]**:

| zone | height above MLLW | what you see |
|---|---|---|
| 1 supralittoral fringe | +3.0 to +1.8 m (+10 to +6 ft) | orange/yellow lichens (Caloplaca, Xanthoria) at top; a wide blackish Verrucaria band below them; small barnacles (Chthamalus, Balanus glandula), periwinkles |
| 2 high intertidal | +1.8 to +1.2 m | rockweed (Fucus gardneri/distichus, dies back to a crust in winter), Mastocarpus (warty lobes and tar spots), larger barnacles (Semibalanus) |
| 3 mid intertidal | +1.2 to 0 m | Ulva (green sheets), dark red algae, aggregating anemones, mussels; tide pools |
| 4 low | below 0 m | kelps (Hedophyllum etc.), surfgrass; bull kelp beds offshore to ~20-30 m depth |

Exposed shores (west side of San Juan Island, outer coast) carry the bands higher: splash lifts
them. I lift them by 0.6 x an exposure factor (0 sheltered .. 1 open swell) **[mine, not measured]**.
Boundaries: the upper edge of each band is crisp but wanders with the rock (+-0.3 m over ~3 m); the lower
edge thins out over ~0.25 m **[mine, from the rocky-intertidal photos]**.

### 1.7 Bull kelp and tidal current

Nereocystis: stipe 10-36 m, one float (pneumatocyst) 9-15 cm across, 30-64 blades up to 4-10 m;
annual: appears in spring, canopy peaks late summer, torn out by winter storms; forests in <30 m on
rock **[source: Wikipedia; Encyclopedia of Puget Sound; seaweed floras]**. Visible canopy area
shrinks 22.5% per metre of tide rise in slack water and 35.5% per metre where currents exceed
10 cm/s, because current drags the kelp diagonally under **[source: Timmer et al. 2024, Frontiers
in Environmental Science, BC central coast]**.

### 1.8 Surf around sea stacks (Olympic coast)

- Wave climate: 1.2 (measured NDBC 46041). Deep-water wavelength L0 = 1.56 T^2.
- Refraction: crests are lines of equal travel time with celerity c = sqrt(g d) in shallow water,
  so they bend toward the shore and wrap around stacks. Breaking at H ~ 0.78 d (depth-limited).
  My refraction solve on a 1:40 beach with winter Hs 3 m breaks ~150 m from shore and stays
  saturated across the surf zone **[measured on my solve]**.
- Breaker type by Iribarren number xi = tan(beta)/sqrt(H/L0) **[source: Battjes 1974]**: spilling
  < 0.5 (Olympic sand beaches: wide dissipative surf zones with many lines of bores), plunging
  0.5-3.3 (reefs, steeper shelves), surging > 3.3 (steep rock faces: surge and burst).
- Runup on dissipative beaches R ~ 0.043 sqrt(H0 L0) **[source: Stockdon et al. 2006]**: winter
  ~1.2 m vertical, tens of metres of swash on a 1:40 beach: the wide shining wet-sand band.
- Photos: Cannon Beach from the bluff (~20 deg down): many thin broken crest lines, each trailing a
  soft bore shoreward; lace between; a wide mirror band of wet sand; white collars on every rock.
  Point of the Arches: shoreward wave faces translucent

Bash: Build contact sheets of 2016 Olympic obliques
mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/{src,refs,sheets,stages,notes}; cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea; cat > src/contact.py <<'EOF'
import sys,glob,os
from PIL import Image,ImageDraw
# usage: contact.py out.png cols thumbw files...
out,cols,tw=sys.argv[1],int(sys.argv[2]),int(sys.argv[3]);fs=sys.argv[4:]
th=int(tw*2/3);rows=(len(fs)+cols-1)//cols
S=Image.new('RGB',(cols*tw,rows*(th+12)),(20,20,24));d=ImageDraw.Draw(S)
for i,f in enumerate(fs):
    im=Image.open(f).convert('RGB');im.thumbnail((tw,th))
    x,y=(i%cols)*tw,(i//cols)*(th+12);S.paste(im,(x,y+12));d.text((x+2,y),os.path.basename(f)[:40],fill=(220,220,220))
S.save(out)
EOF
O=~/work/voxsim-data/places/olycoast/obliques
nice python3 src/contact.py notes/oly_2016_a.png 5 360 $(ls $O/*_2016.jpg | head -40)
nice python3 src/contact.py notes/oly_2016_b.png 5 360 $(ls $O/*_2016.jpg | tail -n +41 | head -40)
ls $O/*_2016.jpg | wc -l

# output:
"""surf.py -- the Olympic coast: swell breaking around sea stacks, over reefs and on sand beaches,
filling surge channels. The camera looks seaward (+y), so we see the shoreward faces of waves.

Physics (sources and measured numbers in LESSONS 1.2 and 1.8):

  * wave climate from NDBC 46041 (measured): winter Hs ~3 m, Tp ~12-13 s; summer ~1.1 m, 8 s;
    storms 8-10 m.
  * refraction: crests are lines of equal travel time from offshore, T(x) with |grad T| = 1/c,
    c = sqrt(g d) in shallow water and g T/2pi in deep; solved as a shortest path (Dijkstra on the
    grid, 16-neighbour stencil). Crests slow over shoals, bend toward the shore and wrap around stacks.
  * shoaling and breaking: H grows as d^-1/4 (Green's law) until H = 0.78 d (depth-limited breaking),
    then stays saturated at gamma d across the surf zone (spilling on flat sand: a wide dissipative
    zone with many lines of bores); on steep rock, waves surge and burst without breaking first.
  * sheltering: behind a stack, energy arriving along the wave direction is blocked; the lee is calm
    except for wrapped (refracted/diffracted) crests.
  * runup on a dissipative beach R ~ 0.043 sqrt(H0 L0) (Stockdon et al. 2006): winter 3 m / 13 s ->
    ~1.2 m vertical -> tens of metres of swash on a 1:40 beach, the wide wet-sand mirror band.
  * foam: bores leave a sheet that decays into lace over ~10-40 s; collars cling to every exposed
    rock; surge channels fill with lace and drain with each wave.
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy.sparse import csgraph, coo_matrix
from noise import fbm, vnoise, hash01, smoothstep, ridged, gauss_blur
from render import World
from rapid import Boulder, boulder_height

G_ = 9.81

CLIMATE = {'summer': dict(Hs=1.1, T=8.3), 'mean': dict(Hs=2.0, T=11.0), 'winter': dict(Hs=3.0, T=12.9),
           'storm': dict(Hs=7.0, T=15.0)}


class SurfSite:
    def __init__(self, sea='winter', tide=1.0, seed=7, beach_slope=1 / 40, stacks=None, wind=0.3, swell_dir=22.0):
        self.sea = sea
        c = CLIMATE[sea] if isinstance(sea, str) else sea
        self.Hs, self.T = c['Hs'], c['T']
        self.tide = tide                           # water level above MLLW (m); range ~ -0.5 .. 3
        self.seed, self.slope, self.wind = seed, beach_slope, wind
        self.swell_dir = swell_dir                  # degrees from shore-normal (Olympic winter swell: W-NW)
        rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
        # sea stacks: (x, y, radius, height). The shoreline runs along y ~ 0..40 (beach), sea toward +y.
        self.stacks = stacks or [(-60, 130, 14, 38), (35, 95, 8, 26), (80, 170, 18, 45), (5, 190, 6, 18),
                                 (-110, 70, 10, 30), (130, 60, 7, 16)]
        self.rocks = []
        for i in range(90):                         # reef and beach rocks, clustered near stacks
            if rng.random() < 0.7:
                sx, sy, sr, sh = self.stacks[rng.integers(len(self.stacks))]
                a = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi); d = sr * rng.unifor

Bash: Read Ruskin on foam and sea painting

Sec. 29. Sea painting. Impossibility of truly representing foam.

More determined efforts have at all periods been made in sea painting
than in torrent painting, yet less successful. As above stated, it is
easy to obtain a resemblance of broken running water by tricks and
dexterities, but the sea _must_ be legitimately drawn; it cannot be
given as utterly disorganized and confused, its weight and mass must be
expressed, and the efforts at expression of it end in failure with all
but the most powerful men; even with these few a partial success must be
considered worthy of the highest praise.

As the right rendering of the Alps depends on power of drawing snow, so
the right painting of the sea must depend, at least in all coast
scenery, in no small measure on the power of drawing foam. Yet there are
two conditions of foam of invariable occurrence on breaking waves, of
which I have never seen the slightest record attempted; first the thick
creamy curdling overlapping massy form which remains for a moment only
after the fall of the wave, and is seen in perfection in its running up
the beach; and secondly, the thin white coating into which this
subsides, which opens into oval gaps and clefts, marbling the waves over
their whole surface, and connecting the breakers on a flat shore by long
dragging streams of white.

It is evident that the difficulty of expressing either of these two
conditions must be immense. The lapping and curdling form is difficult
enough to catch even when the lines of its undulation alone are
considered; but the lips, so to speak, which lie along these lines, are
full, projecting, and marked by beautiful light and shade; each has its
high light, a gradation into shadow of indescribable delicacy, a bright
reflected light and a dark cast shadow; to draw all this requires labor,
and care, and firmness of work, which, as I imagine, must always,
however skilfully bestowed, destroy all impression of wildness,
accidentalism, and evanescence, and so kill the sea. Again, the openings
in the thin subsided foam in their irregular modifications of circular
and oval shapes dragged hither and thither, would be hard enough to draw
even if they could be seen on a flat surface; instead of which, every
one of the openings is seen in undulation on a tossing surface, broken
up over small surges and ripples, and so thrown into perspectives of the
most hopeless intricacy. Now it is not easy to express the lie of a
pattern with oval openings on the folds of drapery. I do not know that
any one under the mark of Veronese or Titian could even do this as it
ought to be done, yet in drapery much stiffness and error may be
overlooked; not so in sea,--the slightest inaccuracy, the slightest want
of flow and freedom in the line, is attached by the eye in a moment of
high treason, and I believe success to be impossible.

Yet there is not a wave or any violently agitated sea on which both
these forms do not appear,

# Painting shallow water that mirrors the sky

*By the channel student. The story of how I learned this, dead ends included, is in [POST.md](POST.md); my raw working thread is [the notebook](../../notebooks/channel/notebook.md).*

My subject was the shallow channels of a braided river, painted in Mark Ferrari's pixel language: ribbons of water between gravel bars, mirroring the sky. What worked in my study was **the water**: a copy of his V02 river ribbons that passed the blind bar, a still mirror the critic called the best water in the studio, and a low dusk channel that was judged my best image. So that's what I teach here, as eight how-tos. Each one works from the real code and the real images that got me there.

![the low dusk channel](img/fig_low_dusk_4x.png)

*A channel on a braided plain at dusk, 256×144 native pixels at 4×. `render_designed('low', 'dusk')` in `code/s5_braid.py`.*

What I'm *not* teaching: gravel, grass and boulders. I needed them around my water, but mine were the weak parts (the critic's word for my gravel was "camouflage"), and other students studied them properly. You'll see my versions in the pictures. Take the water from here and the rest from them.

---

## The one idea

**Water has almost no appearance of its own. Every water pixel is chosen by a rule about something else.**

- The open mirror is **the sky's own colour ramp**, stepped by the elevation of the reflected ray, so nearer water mirrors higher sky. I found this in his index files, and it's true in the photos.
- A reflection is **the painted pixels of what the ray hits**, copied and dropped a step. It's never re-rendered.
- The edges are **the geometry of the bank**: the near edge is light, the far edge is dark.
- The colour is **the palette**: one grey index canvas, re-lit per hour.

What the water adds of its own is small: a checker as its material, a few tints where you see the bed, and some deliberate marks.

## The how-tos

1. **[Read the water out of the master's own files](howto/01-read-his-water.md).** Find his nearest passage anywhere in his work, print its index map as text, measure it, and copy it by rules until it passes. Then trace his shapes, so the test is your procedure. This is where every rule below came from.

   ![copy A](img/copyA_v12.png)

2. **[The mirror as a material](howto/02-the-mirror-as-a-material.md).** A physical value (Fresnel, sky at the reflected elevation, the bed fading into milk), quantized onto the sky ramp with a *checker* quantizer. Then the sheen, the climb with nearness, clouds above the frame, and solid wind lines, each shown switched off.

   ![quantizers](img/fig_quantizers.png)

3. **[What the mirror sees](howto/03-what-the-mirror-sees.md).** A heightfield, raycast one screen column at a time, because a reflected ray stays in its column. Reflections copy the painted pixel at the hit and apply the tone law. Objects mir
Bash: Read channel tells and aerial tutorial
cd ~/work/artschool/posts; cat channel/howto/08-tells.md | head -120; cat aerial/TUTORIAL.md

# output:
# 8. The tells: eighteen ways my pixel water gave itself away

[← back to the front page](../TUTORIAL.md)

**What it's for.** Every image here is one I actually made, with the critic's word for it where there was one, why it happened, and what fixed it. I'd read this page before painting any water: most of these tells come from a rule that is *correct* but was applied in one row, one line, or at one spacing.

Three families cover almost all of them:

- **a line where there should be a material:** one row of checker, a ruled diagonal, a dotted edge;
- **a period where there should be a variety:** even spacing, equal lengths, one bump repeated;
- **a primitive where there should be a form:** a perfect dome, a V, a polygon.

---

## Lines where there should be a material

**1. Morse code.** A single row of 50% checker reads as a dotted line, never as a blend.

![Morse](../img/nb055.png)

*nb055: the far meniscus, the reflected foot and the near lap line, each one dotted row.* **Fix:** a flat tint with a stepped boundary (runs of 3–16 px), or a value change that a texture carries. A checker is only a material over several rows. ([how-to 4](04-edges.md))

**2. The ruled diagonal.** A long straight 45° edge is the fastest giveaway in a copy.

![ruled](../img/nb032.png)

*nb032, copy A v4: the glitter band's lower edge and the dark wedge under it are one straight line at 3×.* **Fix:** the edge dies out through thinning marks, with a few specks spilling past it.

**3. The same tell in a new costume.** My fix for 2 was a 45° band of checker, and it read as a zip.

![zipper](../img/nb047.png)

*nb047, copy A v10, lower right.* **Fix:** a short margin with sparse specks, no band. When you fix a tell, look for it again in the fix.

**4. The dotted ruled front.** A riffle's upstream edge drawn as a straight diagonal of single light pixels.

![front](../img/nb122.png)

*nb122.* **Fix:** the lip follows the bed (skewed and bowed), and it's where the marks are *densest*. ([how-to 7](07-breaking-the-mirror.md))

**5. Scanline tearing.** Long flat light rectangles, 6–30 px, over checker.

![tearing](../img/nb124.png)

*nb124.* **Fix:** riffle dashes are 1–5 px, varied in tone, sometimes turning into short arcs.

**6. Water painted like sky.** Flat bands with seams make a painted stripe.

![banded](../img/nb062.png)

*nb062.* **Fix:** the checker quantizer; the checker is the water's material. ([how-to 2](02-the-mirror-as-a-material.md))

## Periods where there should be a variety

**7. The road.** A ribbon of constant width with parallel edges.

![road](../img/nb029.png)

*nb029, copy A v1.* **Fix:** the banks bite into the water in stepped notches 2–4 px deep; the width pinches and swells.

**8. Brick courses.** Gravel as horizontal dashes of similar length, laid in rows.

![bricks](../img/nb054.png)

*nb054, stage 1 v3, the near bank.* **Fix:** stones at random heights, each a light-over-dark pair whose width is its size projected at that row; fill each row by *a
Bash: Read aerial water how-to (coast section)

# output:
# How-to 3: Water as a dark mirror, and the coast by depth

*[Back to the field guide](../TUTORIAL.md). Code: `~/work/pixelart-studies/aerial/lib/tech_water.py`, `water_ramp` in `lib/pal.py`. Studies: `studies/river.png`, `studies/coast.png`.*

## 1. What the photos say

The Cosumnes River drone photo has the same river running from under the drone to the horizon, and it settles the question:

- **Seen steeply from above, a river is darker than everything around it.** L\* 23, almost colourless (C\* 3), against canopy at 53 and a sunny field at 68.
- **Toward grazing angles it turns into sky.** L\* 45, C\* 32, sky blue.
- The far bank's trees hang down into the water as a dark band.
- The near bank has a dark lip. At grazing angles, the far waterline has a light line.
- Flow shows as sparse streaks along the current.

Physics agrees: Fresnel reflectance is 2–5% looking 30–40° down, and 15–20% at 10–15°. The sky is only visible in water when you look across it.

Ferrari, seen from high up (V02), makes water the lightest thing on the land: sky colours in flat bands with checker seams, and a dark 1-px line on each bank. Those two disagree for a steep camera. So you **choose a stylisation on purpose** and say which one you chose.

## 2. The ramp is the sky, not an albedo

Water has no colour of its own to light. Its ramp is built from the light state's sky colours and a dark body (`pal.water_ramp`):



0 shaded bank lip | 1 reflection of land/trees | 2 water body | 3 low-sky sheen | 4 bright sky (grazing) | 5 glint






t, h = light.sky_top, light.sky_hor
b = np.array(body, float) * 0.7 + t * 0.28          # body between the measured tarn (L*46) and steep river (L*23)
cols = [b * 0.45, b * 0.8 + t * 0.05, b * 0.85 + t * 0.25, b * 0.45 + t * 0.6, b * 0.15 + h * 0.9, sun * 0.3 + 255 * 0.7]




Because the ramp is the sky, the evening river is lit pink and violet with no extra code. That's the thread of light down the valley in the evening range plate.

## 3. Trace the reflected ray

For every water pixel, mirror the view ray and march it through the terrain *plus the canopy* (so trees show up in the mirror). If it hits land, the pixel is step 1. If it hits sky, it goes from the body to bright sky by the Fresnel term, brighter toward the horizon, posterised in flat bands with narrow dithered seams:



R = D.copy(); R[:, 2] = -R[:, 2]
P = np.c_[G[&quot;x&quot;][ys, xs], G[&quot;y&quot;][ys, xs], G[&quot;z&quot;][ys, xs] + 0.3]
t = Trefl.raymarch(P[None], R[None], t0=0.5, t1=3000)[0]
land = np.isfinite(t)
el = np.arcsin(np.clip(R[:, 2], 0, 1))                     # sky elevation seen in the mirror
F = np.clip(fresnel(R[:, 2]) * WATER_BOOST, 0, 1)
skyb = 0.55 + 0.45 * (1 - el / (np.pi / 2))
x = 2.0 + np.clip(F * skyb * 5.0, 0, 2.2)
s = np.clip(np.where(land, 1, flat_bands(x, thr, 0.25)), 1, 4)




`WATER_BOOST` is the stylisation. Here is the same river at three values:

![Fresnel boost](../img/fig_water_boost.png)

*`code/figs.py water`. ×1.0 is the physics: darke

Based on the search results, here's information about the Washington DNR floating kelp canopy survey:

**Survey Program:**
The Nearshore Habitat Program has conducted annual aerial surveys of floating kelp canopy along the outer coast and Strait of Juan de Fuca since 1989. Monitoring was expanded to include DNR Aquatic Reserves in 2010 (Smith Island) and 2011 (Cypress Island and Cherry Point).

**Species Monitored:**
Two species of canopy-forming kelp are monitored: Bull kelp (Nereocystis luetkeana) and giant kelp (Macrocystis pyrifera).

**Historical Extent:**
A 2005 report documented a total of approximately 1,700 hectares of floating kelp (Nereocystis and Macrocystis) on Washington's outer coast and the Strait of Juan de Fuca.

**Data Availability:**
Annual kelp inventories on the outer coast and Strait of Juan de Fuca are available from 1989 to 2024 and throughout three DNR Aquatic Reserves: Smith and Minor Islands, Cypress Island, and Cherry Point from 2010 to 2024. The kelp data can be explored interactively.

Based on the search results, here's information about bull kelp canopy aerial appearance, floats, blades, and canopy density:

## Aerial Appearance

From an overhead or satellite view, floating bull kelp shows the bulb and part of the stipe emerged, with the blades submerged in water. The float supports 30 to 64 long, narrow blades that form a golden-brown canopy on the water's surface.

## Floats and Blades

Bull kelp has a flexible stem (stipe) extending 30 to 60 feet, gradually enlarging to form a single bulbous structure (pneumatocyst) that is positively buoyant. The pneumatocyst provides lift to the alga's blades, allowing it to form the dense canopies observed off the Pacific coast.

## Canopy Density

Regarding density measurements, aerial-based monitoring methods used in British Columbia define dense beds as greater than 10 plants per 10 square metres, and sparse as fewer than 10 plants. The density of kelp canopy at the surface affects the reflectance detected by sensors and their ability to discriminate kelp from water.

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea; cat > src/crop.py <<'EOF'
import sys
from PIL import Image
# crop.py src out cx cy w h [scale]  (coords in FULL-res pixels)
s,o=sys.argv[1],sys.argv[2];cx,cy,w,h=map(int,sys.argv[3:7]);sc=float(sys.argv[7]) if len(sys.argv)>7 else 1
im=Image.open(s).convert('RGB');c=im.crop((cx-w//2,cy-h//2,cx+w//2,cy+h//2))
if sc!=1:c=c.resize((int(c.width*sc),int(c.height*sc)),Image.LANCZOS)
c.save(o)

Bash: Load DEM around Point of Arches
mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat > ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src/dem.py <<'EOF'
"""Read the olycoast/sanjuan 1 m pack tiles (float32 1000x1000, NaN nodata, EPSG:26910, NAVD88 m)."""
import numpy as np, zstandard, os, pyproj
ROOT = os.path.expanduser('~/work/voxsim-data/places')
_T = pyproj.Transformer.from_crs(4326, 26910, always_xy=True)
def lonlat_to_utm(lon, lat): return _T.transform(lon, lat)
def tile(place, layer, tx, ty, dtype=np.float32):
    f = f'{ROOT}/{place}/pack/{layer}/{tx}_{ty}.zst'
    if not os.path.exists(f): return None
    b = zstandard.ZstdDecompressor().decompress(open(f, 'rb').read(), max_output_size=1 << 28)
    a = np.frombuffer(b, dtype=dtype)
    n = int(round(np.sqrt(a.size))); return a.reshape(n, n)
def window(place, layer, x0, y0, x1, y1, dtype=np.float32):
    """Mosaic [x0,x1) x [y0,y1) in UTM metres; returns array with row 0 = north (y1)."""
    W, H = int(x1 - x0), int(y1 - y0)
    out = np.full((H, W), np.nan, np.float32)
    for tx in range(int(x0 // 1000), int((x1 - 1) // 1000) + 1):
        for ty in range(int(y0 // 1000), int((y1 - 1) // 1000) + 1):
            t = tile(place, layer, tx, ty, dtype)
            if t is None: continue
            # guess orientation: row 0 = north edge of tile
            ox, oy = tx * 1000, ty * 1000
            ax0, ax1 = max(x0, ox), min(x1, ox + 1000); ay0, ay1 = max(y0, oy), min(y1, oy + 1000)
            if ax0 >= ax1 or ay0 >= ay1: continue
            sub = t[int(oy + 1000 - ay1):int(oy + 1000 - ay0), int(ax0 - ox):int(ax1 - ox)]
            out[int(y1 - ay1):int(y1 - ay0), int(ax0 - x0):int(ax1 - x0)] = sub
    return out
EOF

# output:
59 drape.py
"""Oblique 'photo' of the real tile: NAIP draped on the 1 m DEM, projected exactly like vox-paint
(orthographic, row = t*ty - z*zy per ppm; ty = sin(elev), zy = zf*cos(elev)/cos(30))."""
import numpy as np, sys, math
from PIL import Image
Z=np.load('tile_dem1.npy'); I=np.load('tile_naip05.npy')
X0,Y1=689900,5196400   # tile NW corner (UTM); row 0 = north
def basis_yaw(yaw):
    s,c=math.sin(yaw),math.cos(yaw); return (c,-s),(-s,-c)
def render(cx,cy,mpp,yaw_deg=45,elev=30,zf=1.0,W=640,H=360,step=0.5,out=None,fov=50.0,persp=None,zc=None):
    ppm=1/mpp; R,V=basis_yaw(math.radians(yaw_deg))
    ty=math.sin(math.radians(elev)); zy=zf*math.cos(math.radians(elev))/math.cos(math.radians(30))
    # sample grid in world metres (local: x east from X0, y north from tile south)
    def ss(a,b,x):
        t=min(max((x-a)/(b-a),0),1); return t*t*(3-2*t)
    pw=persp if persp is not None else ss(26,16,elev)
    P=pw*math.tan(math.radians(fov/2))/(W*0.5*mpp) if pw>1e-3 else 0.0
    half=(W*mpp*0.75+H*mpp*2.5) if P==0 else 1500
    step=step if P==0 else 1.0
    n=int(2*half/step)
    xs=cx-half+np.arange(n)*step; ys=cy-half+np.arange(n)*step
    gx,gy=np.meshgrid(xs,ys)
    ok=(gx>=0)&(gx<2999)&(gy>=0)&(gy<2999)
    gx=gx[ok]; gy=gy[ok]
    # bilinear DEM
    col=gx; row=2999-gy
    c0=np.floor(col).astype(int); r0=np.floor(row).astype(int); tc=col-c0; tr=row-r0
    c1=np.minimum(c0+1,2999); r1=np.minimum(r0+1,2999)
    z=(Z[r0,c0]*(1-tc)*(1-tr)+Z[r0,c1]*tc*(1-tr)+Z[r1,c0]*(1-tc)*tr+Z[r1,c1]*tc*tr)
    colr=I[np.clip((row*2).astype(int),0,5999),np.clip((col*2).astype(int),0,5999)]
    if zc is None:   # vox-studio's ground_m: mean ground over a 9x9 grid, step 30*mpp m
        st=max(min(mpp*30,30.0),2.0); acc=[]
        for j in range(-4,5):
            for i in range(-4,5):
                px=min(max(cx+i*st,0),2999); py=min(max(cy+j*st,0),2999); acc.append(Z[int(2999-py),int(px)])
        zc=float(np.mean(acc))
    dx=gx-cx; dy=gy-cy
    s2=math.sqrt(2.0)
    u=dx*R[0]+dy*R[1]; t=dx*V[0]+dy*V[1]
    ox=u*ppm; oy=(t*ty-(z-zc)*zy)*ppm
    if P>0:
        sc=1/(1+np.maximum(-t*s2*P,-0.9))   # (perspective depth in vox-paint's t units: metres * sqrt 2)
        sc=np.where(t*s2 < 0.6/P, sc, 0)   # near clip
        ox=ox*sc; oy=oy*sc
    sx=(ox+W/2).astype(int); sy=(oy+H/2).astype(int)
    m=(sx>=0)&(sx<W)&(sy>=0)&(sy<H)
    X=sx[m]; Y=sy[m]; T=t[m].astype(np.float32); C=colr[m]
    offs=[(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1),(0,2),(1,2)]
    XX=np.concatenate([X+dx for dx,dy in offs]); YY=np.concatenate([Y+dy for dx,dy in offs])
    TT=np.concatenate([T-0.001*(dx+dy) for dx,dy in offs]); CC=np.concatenate([C]*len(offs))
    k=(XX<W)&(YY<H); XX,YY,TT,CC=XX[k],YY[k],TT[k],CC[k]
    order=np.argsort(TT,kind='stable')
    img=np.full((H,W,3),(150,180,215),np.uint8)
    img[YY[order],XX[order]]=CC[order]
    im=Image.fromarray(img)
    if out: im.resize((W*2,H*2),Image.NEAREST).save(out)
    return im
if __name__=='__main__':
    a=sys.argv
    render(float(a[1]),float(a
Bash: Hillshade map of Point of Arches
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat > hill.py <<'EOF'
import numpy as np, sys
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
from dem import *
def hillshade(a):
    gy,gx=np.gradient(a); n=np.dstack([-gx,gy,np.ones_like(a)]); n/=np.linalg.norm(n,axis=2,keepdims=True)
    L=np.array([-0.5,0.5,0.7]);L/=np.linalg.norm(L); return np.clip(n@L,0,1)
def view(lon,lat,half,out,step=1,grid=100):
    x,y=lonlat_to_utm(lon,lat); x0,y0=int(x-half),int(y-half)
    a=window('olycoast','elev',x0,y0,x0+2*half,y0+2*half)[::step,::step]
    h=hillshade(np.nan_to_num(a,nan=0)/step)
    rgb=np.zeros(a.shape+(3,))
    sea=a<0
    d=np.clip(-a/25,0,1)
    rgb[sea]=np.stack([0.2+0.5*h[sea]*(1-d[sea]),0.4+0.5*h[sea]*(1-d[sea]),0.6+0.3*h[sea]],-1)
    land=~sea; rgb[land]=np.stack([h[land]*0.9+0.1*np.clip(a[land]/40,0,1)]*3,-1)*np.array([1,0.95,0.8])
    im=Image.fromarray((rgb*255).astype(np.uint8)); d_=ImageDraw.Draw(im)
    for g in range(0,im.width,grid//step): d_.line([(g,0),(g,im.height)],fill=(255,0,0)); d_.text((g+2,2),str(g*step),fill=(255,255,0))
    for g in range(0,im.height,grid//step): d_.line([(0,g),(im.width,g)],fill=(255,0,0)); d_.text((2,g+2),str(g*step),fill=(255,255,0))
    im.save(out); print('origin NW utm',x0,y0+2*half)
if __name__=='__main__':
    view(float(sys.argv[1]),float(sys.argv[2]),int(sys.argv[3]),sys.argv[4],int(sys.argv[5]) if len(sys.argv)>5 else 1)
EOF

Bash: Detailed cove map with depth classes
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat > hill2.py <<'EOF'
import numpy as np, sys
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
from dem import *
from hill import hillshade
x0,y1=372347,5346016
def crop(c0,r0,c1,r1,out,sc=1,lvl=(-3,0,2)):
    a=window('olycoast','elev',x0+c0,y1-r1,x0+c1,y1-r0)
    h=hillshade(np.nan_to_num(a,nan=0))
    rgb=np.stack([h*0.5+0.2]*3,-1)
    rgb[a<lvl[0]]*=np.array([0.5,0.7,1.0])
    m=(a>=lvl[0])&(a<lvl[1]); rgb[m]=rgb[m]*0.5+np.array([0.1,0.5,0.4])
    m=(a>=lvl[1])&(a<lvl[2]); rgb[m]=rgb[m]*0.5+np.array([0.5,0.4,0.1])
    m=a>=lvl[2]; rgb[m]=rgb[m]*0.6+0.4
    im=Image.fromarray((np.clip(rgb,0,1)*255).astype(np.uint8)).resize((int(a.shape[1]*sc),int(a.shape[0]*sc)),Image.NEAREST)
    d=ImageDraw.Draw(im)
    for g in range((c0//50+1)*50,c1,50): X=(g-c0)*sc; d.line([(X,0),(X,im.height)],fill=(255,0,0) if g%100==0 else (120,0,0)); d.text((X+2,2),str(g),fill=(255,255,0))
    for g in range((r0//50+1)*50,r1,50): Y=(g-r0)*sc; d.line([(0,Y),(im.width,Y)],fill=(255,0,0) if g%100==0 else (120,0,0)); d.text((2,Y+2),str(g),fill=(255,255,0))
    im.save(out)
if __name__=='__main__':
    a=list(map(int,sys.argv[1:5])); crop(*a,sys.argv[5],float(sys.argv[6]) if len(sys.argv)>6 else 1)

Bash: Initial homography registration overlay
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat > reg.py <<'EOF'
"""Register an oblique photo to the DEM's sea plane with a homography from hand-picked pairs.
map coords: (col,row) metres in a frame with NW origin (X0,Y1) UTM. photo coords: full-res px."""
import numpy as np, json, sys
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
from dem import window
def homog(src, dst):
    A=[]
    for (x,y),(u,v) in zip(src,dst):
        A.append([-x,-y,-1,0,0,0,u*x,u*y,u]); A.append([0,0,0,-x,-y,-1,v*x,v*y,v])
    _,_,Vt=np.linalg.svd(np.array(A,float)); H=Vt[-1].reshape(3,3); return H/H[2,2]
def apply(H,x,y):
    p=H@np.stack([x,y,np.ones_like(x)]); return p[0]/p[2],p[1]/p[2]
def overlay(photo, H, X0, Y1, box, out, lvl=0.0, scale=0.4):
    c0,r0,c1,r1=box
    a=window('olycoast','elev',X0+c0,Y1-r1,X0+c1,Y1-r0)
    im=Image.open(photo).convert('RGB'); W,Hh=im.size
    sm=im.resize((int(W*scale),int(Hh*scale)))
    d=ImageDraw.Draw(sm)
    rr,cc=np.nonzero(a>lvl)
    # edge pixels only
    m=a>lvl; e=m&~(np.roll(m,1,0)&np.roll(m,-1,0)&np.roll(m,1,1)&np.roll(m,-1,1))
    rr,cc=np.nonzero(e)
    u,v=apply(H,cc+c0+0.5,rr+r0+0.5)
    for x,y in zip(u*scale,v*scale):
        if 0<=x<sm.width and 0<=y<sm.height: sm.putpixel((int(x),int(y)),(255,0,255))
    for g in range(c0,c1,100):
        rs=np.arange(r0,r1,5.0); u,v=apply(H,np.full_like(rs,g),rs); d.line(list(zip(u*scale,v*scale)),fill=(255,255,0))
    for g in range(r0,r1,100):
        cs=np.arange(c0,c1,5.0); u,v=apply(H,cs,np.full_like(cs,g)); d.line(list(zip(u*scale,v*scale)),fill=(0,255,255))
    sm.save(out)
def warp(photo, H, box, mpp, out=None):
    """photo -> map-space image (top-down), box in map metres."""
    c0,r0,c1,r1=box
    cs=np.arange(c0,c1,mpp)+mpp/2; rs=np.arange(r0,r1,mpp)+mpp/2
    C,R=np.meshgrid(cs,rs); u,v=apply(H,C.ravel(),R.ravel())
    im=np.asarray(Image.open(photo).convert('RGB'))
    ok=(u>=0)&(u<im.shape[1]-1)&(v>=0)&(v<im.shape[0]-1)
    o=np.zeros((C.size,3),np.uint8); o[ok]=im[v[ok].astype(int),u[ok].astype(int)]
    o=o.reshape(C.shape+(3,))
    if out: Image.fromarray(o).save(out)
    return o, ok.reshape(C.shape)
EOF

Each lane gets a cached target dir, and shared data is mounted at `/data`. A warm one-line rebuild of vox-app takes about 14 s and costs well under a cent. Two-minute guide: [tools/remote/README.md](../tools/remote/README.md).

Bash: Thick overlay of rock contours on photo center
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat >> reg.py <<'EOF'
def overlay2(photo, H, X0, Y1, box, crop, out, lvl=0.45, labels=()):
    """draw forward-projected rock mask (above lvl) as translucent magenta on a full-res photo crop."""
    c0,r0,c1,r1=box
    a=window('olycoast','elev',X0+c0,Y1-r1,X0+c1,Y1-r0)
    im=np.asarray(Image.open(photo).convert('RGB')).astype(float)
    x0,y0,x1,y1=crop; sub=im[y0:y1,x0:x1].copy()
    # inverse map each crop pixel to map coords
    Hi=np.linalg.inv(H); yy,xx=np.mgrid[y0:y1,x0:x1]
    c,r=apply(Hi,xx.ravel().astype(float),yy.ravel().astype(float))
    c=c.reshape(xx.shape)-c0; r=r.reshape(xx.shape)-r0
    ok=(c>=0)&(c<a.shape[1]-1)&(r>=0)&(r<a.shape[0]-1)
    z=np.full(xx.shape,-99.0); z[ok]=a[r[ok].astype(int),c[ok].astype(int)]
    m=z>lvl; e=m&~(np.roll(m,2,0)&np.roll(m,-2,0)&np.roll(m,2,1)&np.roll(m,-2,1))
    sub[e]=[255,0,255]
    m3=(z>-3)&(z<=lvl); e3=m3&~(np.roll(m3,1,0)&np.roll(m3,-1,0)&np.roll(m3,1,1)&np.roll(m3,-1,1)); sub[e3]=sub[e3]*0.3+np.array([255,255,0])*0.7
    o=Image.fromarray(sub.astype(np.uint8)); d=ImageDraw.Draw(o)
    for (mc,mr),name in labels:
        u,v=apply(H,np.array([mc],float),np.array([mr],float)); d.text((u[0]-x0,v[0]-y0),name,fill=(255,255,255))
    o.save(out)
EOF

Bash: Measure sea colour, whitewater, kelp vs depth
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat > lab.py <<'EOF'
import numpy as np
def srgb_to_lab(rgb):
    c=np.asarray(rgb,float)/255; c=np.where(c<=0.04045,c/12.92,((c+0.055)/1.055)**2.4)
    M=np.array([[0.4124,0.3576,0.1805],[0.2126,0.7152,0.0722],[0.0193,0.1192,0.9505]])
    xyz=c@M.T/np.array([0.95047,1.0,1.08883])
    f=np.where(xyz>0.008856,np.cbrt(xyz),7.787*xyz+16/116)
    return np.stack([116*f[...,1]-16,500*(f[...,0]-f[...,1]),200*(f[...,1]-f[...,2])],-1)
def lab_to_srgb(lab):
    lab=np.asarray(lab,float); fy=(lab[...,0]+16)/116; fx=fy+lab[...,1]/500; fz=fy-lab[...,2]/200
    f=np.stack([fx,fy,fz],-1); xyz=np.where(f**3>0.008856,f**3,(f-16/116)/7.787)*np.array([0.95047,1,1.08883])
    Mi=np.linalg.inv(np.array([[0.4124,0.3576,0.1805],[0.2126,0.7152,0.0722],[0.0193,0.1192,0.9505]]))
    c=xyz@Mi.T; c=np.where(c<=0.0031308,12.92*c,1.055*np.clip(c,0,None)**(1/2.4)-0.055)
    return np.clip(c*255,0,255)

def samp(f, rects, vw=1600):

    return out

Bash: Write wave ray-tracing module and run on Point of Arches
mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src/sea; cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat > sea/waves.py <<'EOF'
"""Swell over real bathymetry: linear dispersion, ray refraction (Snell), shoaling, ray-tube spreading,
random-wave breaking (Battjes & Janssen 1978). Output fields on the 1 m grid.

Grid convention: arrays [row, col], col = x east, row = y SOUTH (image order). cell = 1 m unless given.
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as nd
G = 9.81

def wavenumber(omega, d):
    """solve omega^2 = g k tanh(k d) (vectorised, Newton on a good start)."""
    d = np.maximum(np.asarray(d, float), 0.05)
    k0 = omega ** 2 / G
    x = k0 * d
    kd = np.where(x < 1, np.sqrt(x) * (1 + x / 6), x)       # start
    kd = np.maximum(kd, 1e-4)
    for _ in range(12):
        t = np.tanh(kd); f = kd * t - x; df = t + kd * (1 - t * t)
        kd = kd - f / df
    return kd / d

def speeds(omega, d):
    k = wavenumber(omega, d); dd = np.maximum(d, 0.05); kd = k * dd
    c = omega / k
    n = 0.5 * (1 + np.where(kd < 20, 2 * kd / np.sinh(np.minimum(2 * kd, 40)), 0))
    return k, c, n * c

def bilinear(A, x, y):
    h, w = A.shape
    x = np.clip(x, 0, w - 1.001); y = np.clip(y, 0, h - 1.001)
    x0 = x.astype(int); y0 = y.astype(int); tx = x - x0; ty = y - y0
    return (A[y0, x0] * (1 - tx) * (1 - ty) + A[y0, x0 + 1] * tx * (1 - ty) +
            A[y0 + 1, x0] * (1 - tx) * ty + A[y0 + 1, x0 + 1] * tx * ty)

def swell(depth, Hs=1.6, T=8.3, dir_from=281.0, gamma=0.73, alpha=1.0, ray_dx=0.5, ds=0.5,
          smooth=4.0, deep_fill=30.0, max_len=None, spread_deg=0.0, seed=0):
    """depth: metres below still water (negative = land), 1 m grid.
    Returns dict of fields: Hrms, Qb (fraction breaking), D (dissipation, m^3/s... relative),
    kx, ky (unit propagation), phase (travel time, s, complex-averaged), hit (ray weight)."""
    h, w = depth.shape
    omega = 2 * np.pi / T
    d = np.where(np.isnan(depth), deep_fill, depth).astype(float)
    ds_ = nd.gaussian_filter(np.maximum(d, 0.05), smooth)
    land = d <= 0.05
    _, C, CG = speeds(omega, ds_)
    C = np.where(land, 0.5, C)
    gy, gx = np.gradient(C)
    # propagation bearing (clockwise from north) -> grid vector (x east, y south)
    b = np.radians((dir_from + 180) % 360)
    kx0, ky0 = np.sin(b), -np.cos(b)
    # launch line: through the grid centre, perpendicular to k, set back so it starts outside the grid
    cx, cy = w / 2, h / 2
    diag = np.hypot(w, h)
    nxv, nyv = -ky0, kx0
    s = np.arange(-diag / 2, diag / 2, ray_dx)
    X = cx - kx0 * diag / 2 + nxv * s; Y = cy - ky0 * diag / 2 + nyv * s
    KX = np.full_like(X, kx0); KY = np.full_like(X, ky0)
    if spread_deg:
        rng = np.random.default_rng(seed); a = np.radians(rng.normal(0, spread_deg, X.size))
        KX, KY = KX * np.cos(a) - KY * np.sin(a), KX * np.sin(a) + KY * np.cos(a)
    n = X.size
    Hrms0 = Hs / np.sqrt(2)
    _, c0, cg0 = speeds(omega, np.array(deep_fill))
    E0 = Hrms0 ** 2 / 8
    F = np.full(n, E0 * cg0 * ray_dx)        # energy flux per tube (rho g dropped)
    alive = np.ones(n, bool); tt = np.zeros(n)
    acc = {k: np.zeros((h, w)) for k in ('wt', 'H', 'Q', 'D', 'kx', 'ky', 're', 'im')}
    steps = int((max_len or diag * 1.05) / ds)
    started = np.zeros(n, bool)
    for it in range(steps):
        inside = (X >= 0) & (X < w - 1) & (Y >= 0) & (Y < h - 1)
        started |= inside
        alive &= ~(started & ~inside)                     # left the grid after entering
        if not alive.any(): break
        dloc = np.where(inside, bilinear(ds_, X, Y), deep_fill)
        hitland = inside & (bilinear(d, X, Y) <= 0.08)
        alive &= ~hitland
        k, c, cg = speeds(omega, dloc)
        # tube width from neighbours
        dxp = np.hypot(np.diff(X), np.diff(Y))
        bw = np.full(n, ray_dx)
        both = np.zeros(n); both[:-1] += dxp; both[1:] += dxp
        cnt = np.zeros(n); cnt[:-1] += 1; cnt[1:] += 1
        bw = both / np.maximum(cnt, 1)
        bw = np.clip(bw, 0.25 * ray_dx, 8 * ray_dx)
        E = F / (np.maximum(cg, 1e-3) * bw)
        Hrms = np.sqrt(8 * np.maximum(E, 0))
        # Battjes-Janssen breaking
        Hm = 0.88 / k * np.tanh(gamma * k * dloc / 0.88)
        r = np.clip(Hrms / np.maximum(Hm, 1e-3), 0, 1.0)
        # solve (1-Q)/(-ln Q) = r^2 by fixed point
        Q = np.where(r > 0.3, r ** 4, 0.0)
        for _ in range(8):
            Q = np.clip(np.exp(-(1 - Q) / np.maximum(r * r, 1e-6)), 0, 1)
        Q = np.where(r >= 1, 1.0, Q)
        D = 0.25 * alpha * Q * Hm ** 2 / T       # rho g dropped
        m = alive & inside
        F = np.where(m, np.maximum(F - D * bw * ds, 0), F)
        # splat
        if m.any():
            xi = X[m].astype(int); yi = Y[m].astype(int)
            ph = omega * tt[m]
            for key, val in (('wt', 1.0), ('H', Hrms[m]), ('Q', Q[m]), ('D', D[m]), ('kx', KX[m]), ('ky', KY[m]),
                             ('re', np.cos(ph)), ('im', np.sin(ph))):
                np.add.at(acc[key], (yi, xi), val)
        # advance (only rays in deep-fill outside grid move straight)
        cc = np.where(inside, c, c0)
        gcx = np.where(inside, bilinear(gx, X, Y), 0); gcy = np.where(inside, bilinear(gy, X, Y), 0)
        dot = gcx * KX + gcy * KY
        KX2 = KX - ds / cc * (gcx - dot * KX); KY2 = KY - ds / cc * (gcy - dot * KY)
        nn = np.hypot(KX2, KY2); KX = np.where(alive, KX2 / nn, KX); KY = np.where(alive, KY2 / nn, KY)
        X = np.where(alive, X + KX * ds, X); Y = np.where(alive, Y + KY * ds, Y)
        tt = np.where(alive, tt + ds / cc, tt)
    wt = acc['wt']
    out = {}
    # normalised convolution fill (diffraction-ish smoothing into ray shadows)
    sig = 3.0
    W = nd.gaussian_filter(wt, sig)
    for key in ('H', 'Q', 'D', 'kx', 'ky', 're', 'im'):
        v = nd.gaussian_filter(acc[key], sig)
        out[key] = v / np.maximum(W, 1e-9)
    # shadows: H fades where no rays arrive (weight small relative to typical)
    typ = np.median(W[W > 0]) if (W > 0).any() else 1
    cover = np.clip(W / (0.5 * typ), 0, 1)
    Wb = nd.gaussian_filter(cover, 12)             # broad diffraction into the lee
    out['H'] = out['H'] * cover + nd.gaussian_filter(out['H'] * cover, 12) / np.maximum(Wb, 1e-6) * Wb * (1 - cover) * 0.6
    out['Q'] *= cover; out['D'] *= cover
    nk = np.hypot(out['kx'], out['ky']) + 1e-9
    out['kx'] /= nk; out['ky'] /= nk
    out['phase'] = np.angle(out['re'] + 1j * out['im'])      # radians of omega * travel time
    out['cover'] = cover; out['hit'] = wt
    for key in list(out):
        out[key] = np.where(land, 0, out[key]).astype(np.float32)
    out['land'] = land
    out['T'] = T; out['Hs'] = Hs
    return out
EOF
cat > sea/place.py <<'EOF'
"""Real places from the voxsim-data packs (1 m topo-bathy lidar)."""
import numpy as np, os, sys
sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))))
from dem import window, lonlat_to_utm
PLACES = {
    # name: (pack, NW origin UTM x, y, box cols0,rows0,cols1,rows1 in that frame, tide NAVD m at photo)
    'poa': ('olycoast', 372347, 5346016, (350, 250, 1650, 1650)),
}
def load(name, tide=0.45):
    pack, X0, Y1, (c0, r0, c1, r1) = PLACES[name]
    z = window(pack, 'elev', X0 + c0, Y1 - r1, X0 + c1, Y1 - r0)
    return dict(z=z, depth=tide - z, tide=tide, origin=(X0 + c0, Y1 - r0), frame=(c0, r0))

import sys, time; sys.path.insert(0,'.')
import numpy as np
from sea.place import load
from sea.waves import swell
from PIL import Image
P=load('poa'); t=time.time()

Bash: Add eikonal phase solve and rerun
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat >> sea/waves.py <<'EOF'

def eikonal(depth, T=8.3, dir_from=281.0, cell=2.0, smooth=6.0, deep_fill=30.0):
    """First-arrival travel time of a plane swell, |grad t| = 1/c, by Dijkstra on a 16-neighbour
    grid with a delayed super-source along the upwind boundary. Wraps around stacks and into lees
    (the lee gets the diffracted first arrival). Returns t (s) on the 1 m grid, and unit k (grad t)."""
    from scipy.sparse import coo_matrix
    from scipy.sparse.csgraph import dijkstra
    h, w = depth.shape
    omega = 2 * np.pi / T
    d = np.where(np.isnan(depth), deep_fill, depth).astype(float)
    ds_ = nd.gaussian_filter(np.maximum(d, 0.05), smooth)
    f = int(cell)
    dc = ds_[::f, ::f]; landc = (d[::f, ::f] <= 0.05)
    H_, W_ = dc.shape
    _, C, _ = speeds(omega, dc)
    slow = np.where(landc, 1e3, 1.0 / C)
    idx = np.arange(H_ * W_).reshape(H_, W_)
    offs = [(0, 1), (1, 0), (1, 1), (1, -1), (1, 2), (2, 1), (1, -2), (2, -1)]
    rows, cols, vals = [], [], []
    for dy, dx in offs:
        y0, y1 = max(0, -dy), H_ - max(0, dy); x0, x1 = max(0, -dx), W_ - max(0, dx)
        a = idx[y0:y1, x0:x1]; b = idx[y0 + dy:y1 + dy, x0 + dx:x1 + dx]
        L = cell * np.hypot(dx, dy)
        wgt = 0.5 * (slow[y0:y1, x0:x1] + slow[y0 + dy:y1 + dy, x0 + dx:x1 + dx]) * L
        rows += [a.ravel(), b.ravel()]; cols += [b.ravel(), a.ravel()]; vals += [wgt.ravel(), wgt.ravel()]
    # super source
    b_ = np.radians((dir_from + 180) % 360); kx0, ky0 = np.sin(b_), -np.cos(b_)
    border = np.zeros((H_, W_), bool); border[0] = border[-1] = True; border[:, 0] = border[:, -1] = True
    yy, xx = np.mgrid[0:H_, 0:W_]
    proj = (xx * kx0 + yy * ky0) * cell
    upwind = border & (proj < np.percentile(proj[border], 60)) & ~landc
    _, c0, _ = speeds(omega, np.array(deep_fill))
    src = H_ * W_
    s_nodes = idx[upwind]; s_w = (proj[upwind] - proj[upwind].min()) / c0 + 1e-3
    rows.append(np.full(s_nodes.size, src)); cols.append(s_nodes); vals.append(s_w)
    A = coo_matrix((np.concatenate(vals), (np.concatenate(rows), np.concatenate(cols))), shape=(src + 1, src + 1)).tocsr()
    t = dijkstra(A, directed=True, indices=src)[:src].reshape(H_, W_)
    t = np.where(np.isfinite(t), t, np.nan)
    tf = nd.gaussian_filter(np.nan_to_num(t, nan=np.nanmax(t)), 1.0)
    t1 = nd.zoom(tf, f, order=1)[:h, :w]
    if t1.shape != (h, w):
        t1 = np.pad(t1, ((0, h - t1.shape[0]), (0, w - t1.shape[1])), mode='edge')
    gy, gx = np.gradient(nd.gaussian_filter(t1, 2.0))
    n = np.hypot(gx, gy) + 1e-9
    return t1.astype(np.float32), (gx / n).astype(np.float32), (gy / n).astype(np.float32)
EOF

import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as nd
def fbm(shape, scale, seed=0, octaves=4, persist=0.5):
    """smooth value-noise fbm on a grid; scale = feature size in cells. Returns ~N(0,1)-ish."""
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    out = np.zeros(shape, np.float32); amp = 1.0; tot = 0
    s = float(scale)
    for o in range(octaves):
        g = rng.standard_normal((int(shape[0] / s) + 3, int(shape[1] / s) + 3)).astype(np.float32)
        z = nd.zoom(g, s, order=3)[:shape[0], :shape[1]]
        if z.shape != shape: z = np.pad(z, ((0, shape[0] - z.shape[0]), (0, shape[1] - z.shape[1])), mode='reflect')
        out += amp * z; tot += amp * amp; amp *= persist; s = max(s / 2, 1.0)
    return out / np.sqrt(tot)
def hash2(a, b, seed=0):
    x = (np.asarray(a, np.int64) * 374761393 + np.asarray(b, np.int64) * 668265263 + seed * 2246822519) & 0xFFFFFFFF
    x = ((x ^ (x >> 13)) * 1274126177) & 0xFFFFFFFF
    return ((x ^ (x >> 16)) & 0xFFFFFF) / float(0xFFFFFF)
EOF
cat > sea/sim.py <<'EOF'
"""The sea state at one instant, from fields a simulation has: depth, bottom, swell (rays + eikonal),
an ambient current steered around land, and three advected foam quantities integrated over the last
few minutes of breaking.

   W  whitewater: rollers and fresh creamy foam (tau ~4 s)       -- Ruskin's 'thick creamy curdling foam'
   F  subsided foam: lace and streaks (tau ~60-300 s)            -- Ruskin's 'thin white coating ... oval gaps'
   A  aeration: bubble cloud under/after a break (tau ~10 s)     -- the jade halo around stacks and reefs
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as nd
from scipy.sparse import coo_matrix
from scipy.sparse.linalg import spsolve
from .waves import speeds
from .noise import fbm, hash2

def bottom_type(z, depth):
    """rock where the 1 m lidar is rough at 2-6 m scales, sand where smooth."""
    zz = np.nan_to_num(z, nan=-30.0)
    hp = zz - nd.gaussian_filter(zz, 4.0)
    rough = np.sqrt(nd.gaussian_filter(hp * hp, 3.0))
    rock = np.clip((rough - 0.08) / 0.12, 0, 1)
    return rock.astype(np.float32), rough.astype(np.float32)

def ambient_current(land, U=(0.0, 0.25), cell=4):
    """potential flow of a uniform surface current around the land (no flow through coasts).
    U = (east, south) m/s. Solved on a coarse grid; returns (u, v) on the full grid."""
    h, w = land.shape
    L = land[::cell, ::cell]; H_, W_ = L.shape
    wet = ~L
    idx = -np.ones((H_, W_), int); idx[wet] = np.arange(wet.sum())
    rows, cols, vals = [], [], []; b = np.zeros(wet.sum())
    yy, xx = np.mgrid[0:H_, 0:W_]
    phi_b = (U[0] * xx + U[1] * yy) * cell
    border = np.zeros_like(wet); border[0] = border[-1] = True; border[:, 0] = border[:, -1] = True
    for (dy, dx) in ((0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)):
        ny, nx = yy + dy, xx + dx
        inb = (ny >= 0) & (ny < H_) & (nx >= 0) & (nx < W_)
        nyc, nxc = np.clip(ny, 0, H_ - 1), np.clip(nx, 0, W_ - 1)
        nb_wet = inb & wet[nyc, nxc]
        m = wet & nb_wet
        rows.append(idx[m]); cols.append(idx[nyc[m], nxc[m]]); vals.append(-np.ones(m.sum()))
        rows.append(idx[m]); cols.append(idx[m]); vals.append(np.ones(m.sum()))
        # outside the grid: Dirichlet via ghost = far-field potential
        mo = wet & ~inb
        rows.append(idx[mo]); cols.append(idx[mo]); vals.append(np.ones(mo.sum()) * 2)
        np.add.at(b, idx[mo], 2 * (phi_b[mo] + (U[0] * dx + U[1] * dy) * cell))
    n = wet.sum()
    A = coo_matrix((np.concatenate(vals), (np.concatenate(rows), np.concatenate(cols))), shape=(n, n)).tocsr()
    phi = np.full((H_, W_), np.nan); phi[wet] = spsolve(A, b)
    phi = np.where(wet, phi, np.nan)
    # gradient on wet cells
    pf = np.where(wet, phi, 0)
    gy, gx = np.gradient(pf, cell)
    ok = wet & np.roll(wet, 1, 0) & np.roll(wet, -1, 0) & np.roll(wet, 1, 1) & np.roll(wet, -1, 1)
    gx = np.where(ok, gx, 0); gy = np.where(ok, gy, 0)
    u = nd.zoom(gx, cell, order=1)[:h, :w]; v = nd.zoom(gy, cell, order=1)[:h, :w]
    u = np.pad(u, ((0, h - u.shape[0]), (0, w - u.shape[1])), mode='edge')
    v = np.pad(v, ((0, h - v.shape[0]), (0, w - v.shape[1])), mode='edge')
    u = nd.gaussian_filter(u, 2); v = nd.gaussian_filter(v, 2)
    return np.where(land, 0, u).astype(np.float32), np.where(land, 0, v).astype(np.float32)

def advect(q, u, v, dt):
    h, w = q.shape
    yy, xx = np.mgrid[0:h, 0:w].astype(np.float32)
    return nd.map_coordinates(q, [yy - v * dt, xx - u * dt], order=1, mode='constant', cval=0.0)

class SeaState:
    def __init__(self, depth, z, waves, T, current=(0.0, 0.25), wind=(0.0, 0.0), seed=1,
                 tau_w=4.0, tau_f=150.0, tau_a=10.0, gamma_i=0.78, n_periods=36, steps_per_T=10,
                 kelp_amount=1.0, tide=0.45):
        self.depth, self.z, self.T = depth, z, T
        self.land = waves['land'] | ~(depth > 0.02)
        h, w = depth.shape
        self.seed = seed
        d = np.nan_to_num(depth, nan=30.0)
        self.d = d
        self.db = nd.gaussian_filter(np.maximum(d, 0.02), 1.2)
        omega = 2 * np.pi / T
        self.k, self.c, self.cg = speeds(omega, nd.gaussian_filter(np.maximum(d, 0.05), 4))
        self.Hrms = waves['H']; self.tt = waves['t']; self.kx = waves['kx']; self.ky = waves['ky']
        self.rock, self.rough = bottom_type(z, d)
        # currents
        u, v = ambient_current(self.land, current)
        # surface drift: wind (3%), Stokes drift and surf-zone shoreward push along k
        k = self.k
        a = self.Hrms / np.sqrt(2) / 2 * np.sqrt(2)            # ~ amplitude of an Hs/2 wave
        stokes = omega * k * a * a
        surf = 0.35 * np.clip(waves['Q'] * 4, 0, 1)             # bores carry surface foam shoreward
        self.u = u + wind[0] * 0.03 + (stokes + surf) * self.kx
        self.v = v + wind[1] * 0.03 + (stokes + surf) * self.ky
        self.u = np.where(self.land, 0, self.u); self.v = np.where(self.land, 0, self.v)
        self.params = dict(tau_w=tau_w, tau_f=tau_f, tau_a=tau_a, gamma_i=gamma_i, n_periods=n_periods,
                           steps_per_T=steps_per_T)
        # along-crest coordinate (for per-crest random heights)
        yy, xx = np.mgrid[0:h, 0:w].astype(np.float32)
        kx0, ky0 = float(np.median(self.kx[~self.land])), float(np.median(self.ky[~self.land]))
        self.s_along = xx * (-ky0) + yy * kx0
        self.g_field = fbm((h, w), 40, seed + 11, octaves=3)          # slow variation of crest height along crest
        self.kelp = self.make_kelp(kelp_amount, tide)

    def make_kelp(self, amount, tide):
        """bull kelp canopy: on rock, 2-18 m deep, in clumpy beds; streams down-current."""
        h, w = self.d.shape
        d = self.d
        hab = self.rock * np.clip((d - 1.5) / 2.0, 0, 1) * np.clip((18 - d) / 6, 0, 1)
        exp_ = np.clip(1.2 - self.Hrms / 1.2, 0, 1)                 # the most exposed heads carry less canopy
        bed = fbm((h, w), 60, self.seed + 3, octaves=2) * 0.8 + 0.4   # bed-scale patchiness (tens of m)
        clump = fbm((h, w), 7, self.seed + 4, octaves=3)              # clumps of plants (3-8 m)
        # stretch clumps along the current: sample clump noise displaced along (u,v)
        un = np.hypot(self.u, self.v) + 1e-6
        acc = np.zeros_like(clump)
        for s in range(-3, 4):
            acc += nd.map_coordinates(clump, [np.mgrid[0:h, 0:w][0] - self.v / un * s * 1.5,
                                              np.mgrid[0:h, 0:w][1] - self.u / un * s * 1.5], order=1, mode='nearest')
        clump = acc / 7
        k = hab * (0.55 + 0.45 * exp_) * amount
        field = k * 1.2 + 0.45 * bed + 0.9 * clump - 1.1
        return np.clip(field * 2.5, 0, 1).astype(np.float32) * (hab > 0.05)

    def crest_break(self, t):
        """q = fraction of a period since the last crest passed; H_inst of that crest; breaking mask."""
        T = self.T
        q = np.mod((t - self.tt) / T, 1.0)
        n = np.floor((t - self.tt) / T)                              # crest index
        # per-crest random multiplier, varying slowly along the crest (Rayleigh-like, mean ~1)
        cell = np.floor(self.s_along / 30.0)
        r1 = hash2(n, cell, self.seed); r2 = hash2(n, cell + 1, self.seed)
        f = (self.s_along / 30.0) - cell; f = f * f * (3 - 2 * f)
        u = r1 * (1 - f) + r2 * f
        g = np.sqrt(-np.log(np.clip(1 - u * 0.985, 1e-4, 1))) * 1.0 + 0.12 * self.g_field
        Hinst = self.Hrms * g * np.sqrt(1.0)
        Hm = self.params['gamma_i'] * self.db
        brk = (Hinst > Hm) & ~self.land
        return q, Hinst, brk

    def run(self, t_end=None, verbose=False):
        T = self.T; P = self.params
        nst = P['n_periods'] * P['steps_per_T']; dt = T / P['steps_per_T']
        t_end = t_end if t_end is not None else nst * dt
        h, w = self.d.shape
        W = np.zeros((h, w), np.float32); F = np.zeros_like(W); A = np.zeros_like(W)
        rng = np.random.default_rng(self.seed)
        t0 = t_end - nst * dt
        for i in range(nst + 1):
            t = t0 + i * dt
            q, Hinst, brk = self.crest_break(t)
            passed = q * T < dt                                     # crest swept over x in this step
            age = q * T
            src = brk & passed
            # decay
            W *= np.exp(-dt / P['tau_w']); A *= np.exp(-dt / P['tau_a'])
            lost = W * (1 - np.exp(-dt / P['tau_w']))
            F = F * np.exp(-dt / P['tau_f']) + 0.9 * lost
            W = np.where(src, np.maximum(W, np.exp(-age / P['tau_w'])), W)
            A = np.where(src, np.maximum(A, 1.0), A)
            if i % 2 == 0:
                F = advect(F, self.u, self.v, 2 * dt); A = advect(A, self.u, self.v, 2 * dt)
            W = np.where(self.land, 0, W); F = np.where(self.land, 0, F); A = np.where(self.land, 0, A)
            if verbose and i % 50 == 0: print(i, nst, float(W.mean()), float(F.mean()))
        self.W, self.F, self.A = W, np.clip(F, 0, 3), A
        self.q, self.Hinst, self.brk = self.crest_break(t_end)
        self.t = t_end
        return self
EOF

The Wave
In this chapter we will look at animating a wave. I have chosen to
do this because waves are driven by a fundamental energy pattern
that exists in all the elements, the understanding of which will help
us understand how to animate all of the other elements. Wave action
is found everywhere in nature, whether it be water, ﬁre, smoke, or
grass blowing in the wind. Even character animators must understand
the principles of wave animation, as it can be present in a character
gesturing dramatically, or in the ﬂow of a character’s hair or clothing.
Elemental Magic, Volume II

One of the most common places where we see a wavelike motion
occurring is in the ﬂapping of a ﬂag in the wind. It is also one of the
most common tests that students of animation are required to do in any
animation program. Sometimes it is a “wagging dog tail” assignment,
or a “ﬂapping ﬂag” assignment. In any case, it is a vital step in
understanding how things of all kinds move. Virtually everything that
can move, and that’s pretty much everything, will move with some
amount of wave action, however subtle. Even the tectonic plates of the
earth’s crust move in waves. Any physicist will tell you that waves drive
much of all the energy we perceive. Sound, light, the ocean, wind, and
all the elements around us manifest themselves as waves in one way or
another. That is why I have started out with this element—in this case, a
wave of water.
There are a wide variety of different types of waves, each of which has
its own particular characteristics and behaviors. Although all waves
share the same basic principles of how they move from one place to
another, different subsets of waves are created differently, or resolve
themselves differently. For example, if we throw a rock into a pond, the
splash that is created will have waves emanating outward from the point
of impact. These small waves are usually referred to as ripples. Another
example is a boat traveling through the water, creating what we call a
wake: a series of waves emanating outward from behind the boat.

# The open sea and surf from the air: a student's notebook

*Work in progress, written as I go so a crash loses nothing.*

Subject: sea colour, swell, surf lines, foam around stacks, kelp, for the Olympic coast and the San Juans,
at voxsim's scales (close scene ~0.1 m/px, 30° iso at 0.3–1 m/px, region ~1–3 m/px, and a shore
eye-level view). Everything is made by `src/` (Python, numpy + scipy). Tags: **[measured]** = I computed it
from data or photos; **[source]** = from the cited text; **[mine]** = my reading, untested.

## 0. The model systems I stand on

Three traditions, each doing one job:

1. **Linear wave theory as the engineers draw it:** the US Army *Shore Protection Manual* (1984, vol. 1,
   ch. 2; now in `library/water/`) and Bascom's *Waves and Beaches* (1964; lending-only on the Internet
   Archive). Refraction diagrams, orthogonals and their spacing, shoaling, the breaker-type chart. My swell
   is literally an SPM refraction diagram: rays launched offshore, bent by Snell's law over the real
   bathymetry, and wave height from the spacing of neighbouring orthogonals (K_r = sqrt(b0/b)).
2. **Ruskin's two foams** (*Modern Painters* I, part II sec. V ch. III §29–30, 1843): "the thick creamy
   curdling overlapping massy form which remains for a moment only after the fall of the wave", and "the thin
   white coating into which this subsides, which opens into oval gaps and clefts, marbling the waves over
   their whole surface, and connecting the breakers on a flat shore by long dragging streams of white." Plus,
   on heavy sea: "the grand division of the whole space of the sea by a few dark continuous furrows", and the
   flatness of all its lines. These became my two foam fields, W and F.
3. **Gilland, *Elemental Magic* vol. II, "The Wave" and "The Splash"** (2012; `library/purchased/`). His
   *Anatomy of a Reef Break* block diagram says the thing the coast painter misses: **"the outline of foam
   shapes is usually a visual mirror of the reef and sandbar shapes below."** Also: the wave face's
   mid-range shadow where it rises above the waterline, sparkles giving the sun's direction, and the
   principle of chaos — no two sets alike, variation mattering more the closer you are. His splash decay
   (a sheet whose holes expand and intersect into "fantastic fractal shapes", then strings of droplets) is
   the same process as Ruskin's oval gaps, one scale down.

Beside them: Tyndall on the colour of sea water (blue where clear, green where it carries particles), the
Beaufort scale as the sea-state vocabulary, and WA Ecology's shoreline obliques as the answer key.

(The earlier *water* study in `elements/water/` covered surf around stacks at a first pass, and its critic
rounds are the list of what not to do: ruled parallel bands, olive water, direction-less confetti foam,
foam darker than the sea, screen-door checker, thick cream bore stripes. I read them first.)

## 1. Observation

### 1.1 The answer key: WA Ecology obliques registered onto the lidar

The obliques (`~/work/voxsim-data/places/olycoast/obliques`, 4200×2800, ~0.1–0.25 m/px near the frame
centre) are taken from a low aircraft at ~45° down. The 1 m topo-bathymetric lidar in the olycoast pack
reaches ~27 m deep, so the reefs, stacks and sand under the water are known. **The sea is a plane**, so a
homography maps the photo's sea surface exactly onto the map (`src/reg.py`): I picked five stack/rock
pairs by eye for Point of the Arches (photo 060819_15405, 11 Aug 2016 14:27 PDT) and warped the photo into
a top-down "orthophoto" of the cove (`notes/poa_ortho.png`, contours in `notes/poa_ortho_contours.png`).
Registration is good (±5–10 m) near the centre rock, worse (30–60 m) along the east shore: five pairs is
too few. Conditions at the photo **[measured]**: NDBC 46087 (Neah Bay) Hs 1.57 m, Tp 8.3 s, from 277–286°,
wind 1–3.5 m/s NW; La Push gauge 0.40–0.45 m NAVD88 (≈ MLLW −0.5 m, a low tide).

### 1.2 Sea colour **[measured]**, sunlit, summer, L\*a\*b\* medians (`src/sample.py`, `notes/colour_samples.json`)

| what | L\* | a\* | b\* | where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| offshore, 15–20 m | 36 | −9 | −16 | Point of Arches |
| over sand, 3–5 m | 51–53 | −14 to −16 | −11 to −13 | P. of Arches cove, Cape Alava shoal |
| over sand, 6–10 m | 46 | −19 | −7 | Cape Alava |
| over rock reef, clear, ~2–4 m | 53 (sd 7) | −1 | −17 | Cape Alava |
| over rock, ~10 m | 41 | 0 | −25 | Cape Alava |
| kelp mat (surface canopy) | 29 (p10–p90 23–45) | +3 | −20 | Cape Alava |
| kelp speck field (scattered floats) | 37 | −8 | −18 | P. of Arches |
| aerated water by a stack (bubble plume) | 64 (sd 18) | −10 | −10 | P. of Arches |
| foam streak core | 84 | −4 | −9 | P. of Arches |
| surf-zone foam sheet (big swell) | 96 | −2 | 0 | Shi Shi 2006 |
| jade water between foam, big swell | 46 (sd 12) | −17 | −8 | Shi Shi 2006 |
| open sea near a river mouth | 41 | −12 | −8 | La Push |
| sheltered cove by the estuary | 35 | −15 | −4 | La Push |
| estuary (turbid) | 24 | −9 | 0 | Quillayute |

Registered depth series, P. of Arches cove (water pixels only, `notes/poa_ortho.png`):
L\* 50 at 1–6 m, 46 at 7–10 m, 43 at 10–14 m, 40 at 14–20 m; a\* ≈ −11 throughout; b\* ≈ −15.

Readings:

- **Depth lightens, bottom tints.** Sand makes shallow water greener (a\* more negative, b\* less
  negative: yellow sand under blue water); rock makes it bluer and textured; kelp makes it near-black blue.
- **The deep sea is not navy here.** Sunlit summer Olympic water is a mid blue-teal (L\* 36–41), because
  it is productive coastal water (Tyndall: particles make the sea green). Navy only appears in the lee of
  land under high sun or in the San Juans' deep channels.
- **Foam is cool.** Foam reflects the sky: b\* −2 to −9 in sun. The water study's critic already found
  "foam never darker than the sea" and "bore fronts cool white, not cream".

### 1.3 How surf sits on the sea (looked, frame by frame)

Point of Arches, summer swell, low tide (`notes/poa_stack.png`, `notes/poa_streak.png`):

- **Breaks are patches, not rings.** Over a rocky cove the white water is a scatter of short crescents
  and boils, each over a reef head shallower than ~2 m, each with a pale jade halo. Between them is plain
  sea. (The coast painter draws concentric rings by distance to shore; the photo draws the reef.)
- **Stacks:** a white rim tight on the seaward faces, a jade pool of aerated water 1.5–2 stack diameters
  across, thin white filaments curling around the lee.
- **Drifting streaks:** long bands (100–250 m) of old foam cross deep water. Each has a crisp, bright
  leading edge on the shoreward side (the remnant of the bore front) and a soft stippled tail that
  dissolves seaward. They start at reef breaks and are carried off by the current, like smoke from a
  chimney. That is Ruskin's second foam, drawn out.
- **Surface:** fine ripple texture, low contrast; the swell itself is barely visible from the air except
  as broad value undulations.

Cape Alava (`notes/ca_kelp.png`): a kelp mat is a clotted dark patch of lobes 3–8 m across with turquoise
channels between and a ragged edge that breaks into satellite blobs; white boils appear inside the mat
where waves break over rocks under it.

Shi Shi, August 2006, bigger swell (`notes/shi_shi__160811_07663_2006_v.jpg`): the surf zone is saturated —
foam cover > 70%, with jade water showing through oval holes (Ruskin's marbling), bore fronts as brighter
ridges, and the swash as thin white fronts on glassy wet sand.

Morning (10:40 PDT) looking toward the sun (2002 set): the whole cove becomes a silver glitter field cut by
dark ripple lines, and the colour disappears.

Bash: Write camera and G-buffer renderer
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/sea/src; cat > sea/render.py <<'EOF'
"""voxsim's camera: orthographic, 30 deg (row = t*sin(e) - (z - zc)*zf*cos(e)/cos(30)), yawed.
Plus a perspective eye-level camera for the shore view. Produces a G-buffer: world x, y (1 m grid
coords, col/row south-positive), z, sea mask, and land normal."""
import numpy as np, math
from .waves import bilinear

class Cam:
    def __init__(self, cx, cy, mpp, W=640, H=360, yaw=0.0, elev=30.0, zf=1.0, zc=0.0):
        self.cx, self.cy, self.mpp, self.W, self.H = cx, cy, mpp, W, H
        self.yaw, self.elev, self.zf, self.zc = yaw, elev, zf, zc
        a = math.radians(yaw)
        # screen-right and screen-down(ground) unit vectors in grid coords (x east, y south).
        # yaw=0: camera looks north (up the grid): right = +x, forward-down-screen = +y (south = near)
        self.R = (math.cos(a), math.sin(a)); self.V = (-math.sin(a), math.cos(a))
        self.ty = math.sin(math.radians(elev)); self.zy = zf * math.cos(math.radians(elev)) / math.cos(math.radians(30))
    def project(self, x, y, z):
        dx, dy = x - self.cx, y - self.cy
        u = dx * self.R[0] + dy * self.R[1]; t = dx * self.V[0] + dy * self.V[1]
        return u / self.mpp + self.W / 2, (t * self.ty - (z - self.zc) * self.zy) / self.mpp + self.H / 2, t
    def unproject_plane(self, px, py, z):
        u = (px - self.W / 2) * self.mpp
        t = ((py - self.H / 2) * self.mpp + (z - self.zc) * self.zy) / self.ty
        x = self.cx + u * self.R[0] + t * self.V[0]; y = self.cy + u * self.R[1] + t * self.V[1]
        return x, y

def gbuffer(cam, zgrid, sea_level=0.0, ss=2, land_min=None):
    """zgrid: land/sea-floor elevation (NaN = deep). Sea pixels come from the plane z = sea_level;
    land samples are splatted (painter's order by t) wherever z > sea_level."""
    W, H, mpp = cam.W, cam.H, cam.mpp
    py, px = np.mgrid[0:H, 0:W].astype(np.float64) + 0.5
    sx, sy = cam.unproject_plane(px, py, sea_level)
    G = dict(x=sx, y=sy, z=np.full((H, W), sea_level), sea=np.ones((H, W), bool),
             t=np.full((H, W), -1e9), nx=np.zeros((H, W)), ny=np.zeros((H, W)), nz=np.ones((H, W)))
    # land splat: sample the world on a grid finer than a pixel, over the footprint
    h, w = zgrid.shape
    zz = np.nan_to_num(zgrid, nan=-50.0)
    gyz, gxz = np.gradient(zz)
    step = mpp / ss
    # footprint of the screen on the ground (with margin for tall land)
    corners = [cam.unproject_plane(a, b, sea_level) for a in (0, W) for b in (0, H + 80 / mpp * 0 + 60 / mpp)]
    corners += [cam.unproject_plane(a, b, sea_level + 80) for a in (0, W) for b in (0, H)]
    xs_ = [c[0] for c in corners]; ys_ = [c[1] for c in corners]
    x0, x1 = max(0, min(xs_) - 5), min(w - 1, max(xs_) + 5); y0, y1 = max(0, min(ys_) - 5), min(h - 1, max(ys_) + 5)
    gx = np.arange(x0, x1, step); gy = np.arange(y0, y1, step)
    for j0 in range(0, gy.size, 400):                           # chunk rows to bound memory
        GY, GX = np.meshgrid(gy[j0:j0 + 400], gx, indexing='ij')
        z = bilinear(zz, GX, GY)
        m = z > sea_level + 0.02
        if not m.any(): continue
        GXm, GYm, zm = GX[m], GY[m], z[m]
        u, v, t = cam.project(GXm, GYm, zm)
        iu = np.floor(u).astype(int); iv = np.floor(v).astype(int)
        ok = (iu >= 0) & (iu < W) & (iv >= 0) & (iv < H)
        iu, iv, t, GXm, GYm, zm = iu[ok], iv[ok], t[ok], GXm[ok], GYm[ok], zm[ok]
        # also fill the pixel below (vertical faces stretch downward on screen)
        for dv in (0, 1):
            ivv = np.minimum(iv + dv, H - 1)
            order = np.argsort(t, kind='stable')
            a_, b_ = ivv[order], iu[order]
            tt = t[order] - 1e-3 * dv
            cur = G['t'][a_, b_]
            better = tt >= cur
            a_, b_, o2 = a_[better], b_[better], order[better]
            G['t'][a_, b_] = t[o2]; G['x'][a_, b_] = GXm[o2]; G['y'][a_, b_] = GYm[o2]; G['z'][a_, b_] = zm[o2]
            G['sea'][a_, b_] = False
    # land normals
    L = ~G['sea']
    nx = -bilinear(gxz, G['x'][L], G['y'][L]); ny = -bilinear(gyz, G['x'][L], G['y'][L])
    n = np.sqrt(nx * nx + ny * ny + 1)
    G['nx'][L] = nx / n; G['ny'][L] = ny / n; G['nz'][L] = 1 / n
    return G
EOF
echo ok

ok
